   167	# Strategy agent classes (VT/TF/MR/Noise verbatim from K1262b)
   168	# ============================================================
   169	
   170	class StrategyAgent:
   171	    def update_target_weight(self, t, prices, returns, vix_series, current_weights):
   172	        raise NotImplementedError
   173	
   174	
   175	class VTAgent(StrategyAgent):
   235	        new_w = np.clip(current_weights + changes, 0.0, 1.5)
   236	        return new_w, float(np.sum(changes))
   237	
   238	
   239	class RandomRebalanceAgent(StrategyAgent):
   240	    """(b) ACTIVE control: coherent-block random-direction rebalancer.
   241	
   242	    Tests the alternative hypothesis 'ANY large coordinated trading bloc
   243	    destabilizes the market' — same trading footprint as the matched
   244	    strategy, but direction orthogonal to price/vol history.
   245	
   246	    - Rebalances with probability `freq` per day (matched to the strategy's
   247	      measured fraction of active-rebalance days in the same cell×adoption).
   248	    - On a rebalance day the whole bloc moves coherently by Δw = s·|Δw|,
   249	      s = ±1 with prob 0.5, |Δw| ~ lognormal with mean/std matched to the
   250	      strategy's measured per-day |Δw| (mean-weight change of the bloc).
   251	    - Decisions come from a DEDICATED RNG stream (seed+RR_RNG_OFFSET) and
   252	      never read prices / returns / vix → orthogonality by construction.
   253	    - Weights clipped to [-EXPOSURE_CAP, EXPOSURE_CAP] (TF/MR support);
   254	      RR_VT clipped to [0, EXPOSURE_CAP] to match VT's long-only support.
   255	    """
   256	    def __init__(self, freq, dw_mean, dw_std, long_only=False, cap=EXPOSURE_CAP):
   257	        self.freq = float(min(max(freq, 0.0), 1.0))
   258	        dw_mean = max(float(dw_mean), 1e-8)
   259	        dw_std = max(float(dw_std), 1e-10)
   260	        # lognormal parameterization matched to mean/std of |Δw|
   261	        sigma2 = np.log(1.0 + (dw_std / dw_mean) ** 2)
   262	        self.ln_sigma = float(np.sqrt(sigma2))
   263	        self.ln_mu = float(np.log(dw_mean) - 0.5 * sigma2)
   264	        self.long_only = long_only
   265	        self.cap = cap
   266	        self._rng = None  # dedicated stream, set by caller
   267	
   268	    def update_target_weight(self, t, prices, returns, vix_series, current_weights):
   269	        n = len(current_weights)
   270	        if n == 0:
   271	            return current_weights, 0.0
   272	        rng = self._rng
   273	        if rng.random_sample() >= self.freq:
   274	            return current_weights, 0.0   # no rebalance today
   275	        sign = 1.0 if rng.random_sample() < 0.5 else -1.0
   276	        dw = sign * rng.lognormal(self.ln_mu, self.ln_sigma)
   277	        lo = 0.0 if self.long_only else -self.cap
   278	        new_w = np.clip(current_weights + dw, lo, self.cap)
   279	        demand = float(np.sum(new_w - current_weights))
   280	        return new_w, demand
   281	
   282	
   283	def _build_strategy(treatment, rng_seed, turnover_params=None):
   284	    if treatment == 'VT_baseline':
   285	        return VTAgent(cap=VT_CAP)
   286	    if treatment == 'TF':
   287	        return TFAgent()
   288	    if treatment == 'MR':
   289	        return MRAgent()
   290	    if treatment == 'NoiseControl':
   291	        return NoiseAgent()
   292	    if treatment in ('RR_VT', 'RR_TF', 'RR_MR'):
   293	        tp = turnover_params
   294	        if tp is None:
   295	            raise ValueError(f"{treatment} requires turnover_params")
   296	        agent = RandomRebalanceAgent(
   297	            freq=tp['freq'], dw_mean=tp['dw_mean'], dw_std=tp['dw_std'],
   298	            long_only=(treatment == 'RR_VT'),
   299	        )
   300	        agent._rng = np.random.RandomState((rng_seed + RR_RNG_OFFSET) % (2**31 - 1))
   301	        return agent
   302	    raise ValueError(f"Unknown treatment: {treatment}")
   303	
   304	
   305	# ============================================================
   310	    """One sim. args = (treatment, adoption, seed, kyle_lambda, gamma,
   311	                        turnover_params_or_None, keep_returns_bool)"""
   312	    (treatment, adoption, seed, kyle_lambda, vix_vol_sensitivity,
   313	     turnover_params, keep_returns) = args
   314	
   315	    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
   316	
   317	    n_noise = N_NOISE_FIXED
   318	    n_strategy = int(N_BH_VT_POOL * adoption)
   319	    n_bh = N_BH_VT_POOL - n_strategy
   320	
   321	    strategy = _build_strategy(treatment, seed, turnover_params)
   322	    if isinstance(strategy, NoiseAgent):
   323	        strategy._rng = rng
   324	
   325	    prices = np.zeros(N_DAYS)
   326	    returns = np.zeros(N_DAYS)
   327	    vix_series = np.zeros(N_DAYS)
   328	
   329	    if treatment == 'VT_baseline':
   330	        init_w = min(12.0 / INITIAL_VIX, VT_CAP)
   331	    elif treatment in ('TF', 'MR'):
   332	        init_w = 0.0
   333	    elif treatment == 'NoiseControl':
   334	        init_w = 0.5
   335	    elif treatment in ('RR_VT',):
   336	        init_w = min(12.0 / INITIAL_VIX, VT_CAP)   # same starting bloc as VT
   337	    elif treatment in ('RR_TF', 'RR_MR'):
   338	        init_w = 0.0                                # same starting bloc as TF/MR
   339	    else:
   340	        init_w = 0.0
   341	    strategy_weights = np.ones(n_strategy) * init_w if n_strategy > 0 else np.array([])
   342	    noise_weights = np.ones(n_noise) * 0.5
   343	
   344	    prices[0] = INITIAL_PRICE
   345	    vix_series[0] = INITIAL_VIX
   346	
   347	    strategy_weight_history = np.zeros(N_DAYS)
   348	    strategy_weight_history[0] = init_w
   349	
   350	    ret_buffer = np.zeros(20)
   351	    buffer_idx = 0
   352	    n_nan_events = 0
   353	    n_price_clamp = 0
   354	
   355	    # turnover recording (for (b) matched-control calibration)
   356	    abs_dw_sum = 0.0
   357	    abs_dw_sq_sum = 0.0
   358	    n_rebalance_days = 0
   359	    n_decision_days = 0
   360	
   361	    for t in range(1, N_DAYS):
   362	        # VIX update — VERBATIM K827v3/K1261/K1262/K1262b
   363	        realized_vol_20d = (np.std(ret_buffer) * np.sqrt(252) if t > 1
   364	                            else FUNDAMENTAL_VOL * np.sqrt(252))
   365	        vix_target = VIX_MEAN + vix_vol_sensitivity * max(0, realized_vol_20d - 0.16)
   366	        vix_series[t] = (vix_series[t-1]
   367	                         + VIX_MR_SPEED * (vix_target - vix_series[t-1])
   368	                         + rng.normal(0, VIX_NOISE_STD))
   369	        vix_series[t] = max(9.0, min(80.0, vix_series[t]))
   370	
   371	        net_demand = 0.0
   372	
   373	        if n_strategy > 0:
   374	            prev_mean_w = float(np.mean(strategy_weights))
   375	            new_w, strategy_demand = strategy.update_target_weight(
   376	                t, prices, returns, vix_series, strategy_weights)
   377	            net_demand += strategy_demand
   378	            strategy_weights = new_w
   379	            cur_mean_w = float(np.mean(strategy_weights))
   380	            strategy_weight_history[t] = cur_mean_w
   381	            # record turnover of the bloc (per-agent mean |Δw|)
   382	            adw = abs(cur_mean_w - prev_mean_w)
   383	            n_decision_days += 1
   384	            if adw > TURNOVER_EPS:
   385	                n_rebalance_days += 1
   386	                abs_dw_sum += adw
   387	                abs_dw_sq_sum += adw * adw
   388	        else:
   389	            strategy_weight_history[t] = strategy_weight_history[t-1]
   390	
   391	        # Noise traders — VERBATIM
   392	        noise_changes = rng.normal(0, NOISE_TRADER_STD, size=n_noise)
   393	        noise_weights = np.clip(noise_weights + noise_changes, 0.0, 1.5)
   394	        net_demand += np.sum(noise_changes)
   395	
   396	        # Kyle price formation — VERBATIM
   397	        fundamental_shock = rng.normal(DAILY_DRIFT, FUNDAMENTAL_VOL)
   398	        price_impact = kyle_lambda * net_demand / N_AGENTS
   399	        daily_return = fundamental_shock + price_impact
   400	
   401	        if not np.isfinite(daily_return):
   402	            daily_return = 0.0
   403	            n_nan_events += 1
   404	
   405	        returns[t] = daily_return
   690	
   691	# ============================================================
   692	# Runner
   693	# ============================================================
   694	
   695	def _det_hash(*parts):
   696	    """Deterministic string hash (Python's hash() is per-process randomized
   697	    for str → would break fixed-seed reproducibility)."""
   698	    return zlib.crc32('|'.join(str(p) for p in parts).encode()) % 10**6
   699	
   700	
   701	def _seed_for(treatment, adoption, sim_idx, lambda_idx, gamma_idx):
   702	    """Extends K1262b seed formula with deterministic treatment offset."""
   703	    return (int(adoption * 100000) + sim_idx + 42
   704	            + TF_SCALING * 1000 + MOMENTUM_WINDOW * 10
   705	            + lambda_idx * 100 + gamma_idx * 10
   706	            + TREATMENT_SEED_OFFSET[treatment])
   707	
   708	
   709	def run_batch(treatment, adoption, lambda_idx, gamma_idx, n_sims,
   710	              turnover_params=None, keep_returns=False, pool=None):
   711	    args_list = [
   712	        (treatment, adoption,
   713	         _seed_for(treatment, adoption, i, lambda_idx, gamma_idx),
   714	         LAMBDA_GRID[lambda_idx], GAMMA_GRID[gamma_idx],
   715	         turnover_params, keep_returns)
   716	        for i in range(n_sims)
   717	    ]
   718	    if pool is not None:
   719	        return pool.map(run_single_simulation, args_list)
   720	    return [run_single_simulation(a) for a in args_list]
   790	            grid = ADOPTION_GRID_CELL1 if cell_label == 'cell1_baseline' \
   791	                else ADOPTION_GRID_OTHER
   792	            labels = [f"{a*100:.0f}%" for a in grid]

   828	                      f"({time.time()-t0:.0f}s elapsed)")
   829	
   830	            # ---- stage 2: matched active controls (b) ----
   831	            for rr, src in [('RR_VT', 'VT_baseline'), ('RR_TF', 'TF'),
   832	                            ('RR_MR', 'MR')]:
   833	                tr = {'per_adoption': {}, 'sharpe_sims': {},
   834	                      'matched_to': src}

   838	                                     turnover_params=tp, pool=pool)
   839	                    total_sims += n_sims
   840	                    ci_seed = PATH_BOOT_SEED + _det_hash(cell_label, rr, lbl)
   841	                    summ = summarize_batch(sims, ci_seed)
   842	                    summ['matched_turnover_params'] = tp
   843	                    tr['per_adoption'][lbl] = summ
   844	                    tr['sharpe_sims'][lbl] = [
   845	                        s['sharpe'] for s in sims if s['sharpe'] is not None]
   846	                cell['treatments'][rr] = tr
   847	                print(f"  [{cell_label}] {rr} (matched to {src}) done "
   848	                      f"({time.time()-t0:.0f}s elapsed)")
   849	
   850	            # ---- detector pass (a)(d)(e) ----
   880	            'detector': {

   886	                'threshold_boot_seed': THRESHOLD_BOOT_SEED,
   887	                'applicability_floor': APPLICABILITY_FLOOR,
   888	            },

   898	                'treatment_seed_offset': TREATMENT_SEED_OFFSET,
   899	            },

   904	                'momentum_window': MOMENTUM_WINDOW,

   907	            'seed_formula': ('int(adoption*100000)+sim_idx+42+scaling*1000'
   908	                             '+window*10+lambda_idx*100+gamma_idx*10'
   909	                             '+TREATMENT_SEED_OFFSET[treatment]'),

    10	## 動機：5 HIGH blocking issues → 5 項重設計
    11	
    12	| # | v5 blocking issue | K1471 重設計 |
    13	|---|---|---|
    14	| (a) | P5-style detector「先知 70%」循環校準（calibrated to the standalone VT benchmark） | **外生 sup-Wald 斷點檢定**：對每 (cell, treatment) 的 sim-level Sharpe-vs-φ 曲線做 supremum-Wald 單斷點結構檢定（Andrews 1993 風格），p-value 由 label-permutation null（B=999, fixed seed）；斷點不確定性由 path-level bootstrap（B=500, fixed seed）→ frequency dist + 80% interval。舊 drop>{30/50/70}% 規則降級為 descriptive robustness grid，非 calibration anchor |
    15	| (b) | NoiseControl 固定 0.5 權重幾乎零流量 = strawman falsifier | **Active control（RR_VT / RR_TF / RR_MR）**：coherent-block 隨機方向再平衡 agent。\|Δw\| 分佈（lognormal，mean/std matched）與再平衡頻率 match 同 cell×adoption 下對應 treatment 的實測 turnover（兩階段：stage 1 跑 treatments 記 turnover，stage 2 跑 matched controls）。方向 ±1 等機率、由獨立 RNG stream（seed+10000019），不讀任何 price/vix 歷史 → 與價格/波動正交 by construction。舊 NoiseControl 降為 sanity check |
    16	| (c) | iid pooled 1.26M-day bootstrap CI 低估 SE + 「×500 sims」double counting | **CI 統一 path-level bootstrap**：所有 headline CI（Sharpe / kurtosis / MDD）= M 條 sim-level 統計量的 percentile bootstrap（B=2000, fixed seed）。pooled kurtosis 僅 cell1 報告，用 two-level circular block bootstrap（path 重抽 × path 內 42-day blocks，B=300, fixed seed） |
    17	| (d) | cell1 閾值不可再現（M=500 vs M=200 跳 20%→70%）、grid 太疏無 CI | **cell1 grid 加密**：{10,30,**40**,50,**60**,70,100}%；cells 2–5 = {10,30,**50**,70,100}%。每個 threshold 報 path-level bootstrap CI（斷點 frequency dist + 80% interval）；full run M=500 |
    18	| (e) | cell3 MR baseline Sharpe=−5.56，null 被翻轉計入 MR≤VT 支持證據 | **Applicability gate**：最低 adoption mean Sharpe < −0.5（外生 floor，分析前設定）→ detector 回報 `not_applicable_saturated_loss`，不強行宣稱斷點、不計入 ranking 證據 |
    19	
    20	## 設計不變項（與 K827v3/K1261/K1262/K1262b 完全相同）
    21	
    22	- Market microstructure：N=1000 agents（200 noise 固定 + 800 BH/strategy 池）、N_DAYS=2520、VIX feedback（γ）、Kyle price impact（λ）、noise trader std=0.02 — **verbatim**，重設計差異只來自 detector / control / CI
    23	- OAT cells：cell1 baseline（λ=0.005, γ=200）、cell2 λ_low、cell3 λ_high、cell4 γ_low、cell5 γ_high
    24	- Lookahead 防護：VT 讀 `vix_series[t-1]`；TF/MR signal 用 `returns[t-window:t]`（不含 t）；RR_* 不讀任何價格歷史
    25	- Seeds：sim seeds 沿用 K1262b formula + deterministic treatment offset（RR_* 用 7,000,003 / 7,100,003 / 7,200,003 offset 確保與 matched treatment 不重疊）；permutation / threshold bootstrap / CI bootstrap / block bootstrap 各有固定 base seed；字串 hash 用 `zlib.crc32`（非 Python `hash()`，避免 per-process randomization 破壞再現性）
    26	
    27	## Treatments（7）
    28	
    29	| Treatment | 角色 |
    30	|---|---|
    50	## 成功標準
    51	
    52	1. ✅ Detector 單元測試：合成 break 正確定位、flat curve p>0.05 無 false positive、saturated regime 正確 gate、固定 seed 完全 deterministic
    53	2. ✅ Smoke M=50 全 pipeline 跑通（兩階段 + detector + bootstrap CI + block bootstrap）
    54	3. Full M=500：每 (cell, treatment) 有外生 threshold + p-value + bootstrap interval；RR_* 對照組可區分「策略方向性 feedback」vs「任意大型協同流量」
    55	4. 不改 `paper/*.tex`、不寫 knowledge.json（主線程 post-review 處理）
    56	
    57	## Caveats
    58	
    59	1. Smoke M=50 的 detector 輸出僅驗 code path，閾值數字不可引用（power 不足）
    60	2. RR_* 的 \|Δw\| lognormal 經 clip 後實際分佈略窄於 target（單元測試顯示 mean match 至 ~0.2%）
    61	3. 兩階段設計使 RR_* 的 matched turnover params 來自同 run 的 treatment 實測 — 這是 by-design（control matched to measured footprint），非 leakage：turnover stats 是 treatment 的 ensemble 統計量，不進入 RR 的逐日決策資訊集
    62	4. Permutation test 假設 exchangeability under null（同分佈跨 adoption groups）— 對 mean-shift alternative 是 standard；variance 也隨 adoption 變的情況下 sup-Wald 用 Welch-type denominator 部分緩解

   175	class VTAgent(StrategyAgent):
   176	    """Volatility-targeting (P5 K827v3 rule). Reads VIX at t-1 — no lookahead."""
   177	    def __init__(self, cap=EXPOSURE_CAP):
   178	        self.cap = cap
   179	
   180	    def update_target_weight(self, t, prices, returns, vix_series, current_weights):
   181	        n = len(current_weights)
   182	        if n == 0:
   183	            return current_weights, 0.0
   184	        vt_target = min(12.0 / vix_series[t-1], self.cap)
   185	        demand = (vt_target - current_weights) * n
   186	        return np.full(n, vt_target), float(np.sum(demand))
   187	
   188	
   189	class TFAgent(StrategyAgent):
   190	    """Trend-following. Signal = returns[t-window:t] (excludes t — no lookahead)."""
   191	    def __init__(self, window=MOMENTUM_WINDOW, scaling=TF_SCALING, cap=EXPOSURE_CAP):
   192	        self.window = window
   193	        self.scaling = scaling
   194	        self.cap = cap
   195	
   196	    def update_target_weight(self, t, prices, returns, vix_series, current_weights):
   197	        n = len(current_weights)
   198	        if n == 0:
   199	            return current_weights, 0.0
   200	        momentum = 0.0 if t < self.window + 1 else float(np.sum(returns[t-self.window:t]))
   201	        target = float(np.clip(self.scaling * momentum, -self.cap, self.cap))
   202	        demand = (target - current_weights) * n
   203	        return np.full(n, target), float(np.sum(demand))
   204	
   205	
   206	class MRAgent(StrategyAgent):
   207	    """Mean-reversion (opposite sign of TF)."""
   208	    def __init__(self, window=MOMENTUM_WINDOW, scaling=TF_SCALING, cap=EXPOSURE_CAP):
   209	        self.window = window
   210	        self.scaling = scaling
   211	        self.cap = cap
   212	
   213	    def update_target_weight(self, t, prices, returns, vix_series, current_weights):
   214	        n = len(current_weights)
   215	        if n == 0:
   216	            return current_weights, 0.0
   217	        momentum = 0.0 if t < self.window + 1 else float(np.sum(returns[t-self.window:t]))
   218	        target = float(np.clip(-self.scaling * momentum, -self.cap, self.cap))
   219	        demand = (target - current_weights) * n
   220	        return np.full(n, target), float(np.sum(demand))
   221	
   222	
   223	class NoiseAgent(StrategyAgent):
   224	    """LEGACY sanity control (K1261/K1262b): near-zero net flow."""
   225	    def __init__(self, std=NOISE_TRADER_STD):
   226	        self.std = std
   227	        self._rng = None
   228	
   229	    def update_target_weight(self, t, prices, returns, vix_series, current_weights):
   230	        n = len(current_weights)
   231	        if n == 0:
   232	            return current_weights, 0.0
   233	        rng = self._rng or np.random.RandomState(0)
   234	        changes = rng.normal(0, self.std, size=n)
   235	        new_w = np.clip(current_weights + changes, 0.0, 1.5)
   236	        return new_w, float(np.sum(changes))
   237	
   238	
   239	class RandomRebalanceAgent(StrategyAgent):
   240	    """(b) ACTIVE control: coherent-block random-direction rebalancer.

   480	    G = len(groups)
   481	    best_stat, best_k = -np.inf, None
   482	    sizes = [len(g) for g in groups]
   483	    for k in range(G - 1):
   484	        left = np.concatenate(groups[:k+1])
   485	        right = np.concatenate(groups[k+1:])
   486	        n1, n2 = len(left), len(right)
   487	        if n1 < 2 or n2 < 2:
   488	            continue
   489	        m1, m2 = left.mean(), right.mean()
   490	        v1 = left.var(ddof=1) / n1
   491	        v2 = right.var(ddof=1) / n2
   492	        denom = v1 + v2
   493	        stat = (m1 - m2) ** 2 / denom if denom > 0 else 0.0
   494	        if stat > best_stat:
   495	            best_stat, best_k = stat, k
   496	    return best_stat, best_k
   497	
   498	
   499	def detect_threshold_exogenous(sharpe_by_adoption, adoption_labels,
   500	                               perm_seed, boot_seed):
   501	    """Exogenous structural-break detector on the Sharpe-vs-φ curve.
   502	
   503	    No reference to any pre-known threshold. Procedure fixed ex ante:
   504	      1. Applicability gate (e): baseline (lowest adoption) mean Sharpe <
   505	         APPLICABILITY_FLOOR → 'not_applicable_saturated_loss'.
   506	      2. sup-Wald over interior split points of the ordered adoption grid.
   507	      3. Permutation p-value: permute adoption labels over pooled obs
   508	         (B=PERMUTATION_B, seeded).
   509	      4. If p < DETECTOR_ALPHA and post-break mean < pre-break mean →
   510	         threshold = first adoption level of the post-break regime.
   511	      5. Path-level bootstrap (B=THRESHOLD_BOOT_B, seeded): resample sims
   512	         within each adoption group, recompute argmax split → breakpoint
   513	         frequency distribution + 80% interval.
   514	    """
   515	    groups = [np.asarray(sharpe_by_adoption[lbl], dtype=float)
   516	              for lbl in adoption_labels]
   517	    groups = [g[np.isfinite(g)] for g in groups]
   518	    base_mean = float(groups[0].mean()) if len(groups[0]) else np.nan
   519	
   520	    result = {
   521	        'detector': 'exogenous sup-Wald breakpoint + permutation null',
   522	        'baseline_mean_sharpe': base_mean,
   523	        'applicability_floor': APPLICABILITY_FLOOR,
   524	    }
   525	
   526	    # (e) applicability gate
   527	    if not np.isfinite(base_mean) or base_mean < APPLICABILITY_FLOOR:
   528	        result.update({
   529	            'status': 'not_applicable_saturated_loss',
   530	            'threshold': None, 'p_value': None,
   531	            'note': ('baseline mean Sharpe below applicability floor — '
   532	                     'strategy already in saturated loss regime before the '
   533	                     'lowest adoption level; Sharpe-degradation breakpoint '
   534	                     'detection is uninformative here.'),
   535	        })
   536	        return result
   537	
   538	    sup_obs, k_obs = _sup_wald(groups)
   539	    if k_obs is None:
   540	        result.update({'status': 'degenerate', 'threshold': None, 'p_value': None})
   541	        return result
   542	
   543	    # permutation null (fixed seed)
   544	    pooled = np.concatenate(groups)
   545	    sizes = [len(g) for g in groups]
   546	    rng = np.random.RandomState(perm_seed % (2**31 - 1))
   547	    count_ge = 0
   548	    for _ in range(PERMUTATION_B):
   549	        perm = rng.permutation(pooled)
   550	        pg, pos = [], 0
   551	        for s in sizes:
   552	            pg.append(perm[pos:pos+s]); pos += s
   553	        s_perm, _ = _sup_wald(pg)
   554	        if s_perm >= sup_obs:
   555	            count_ge += 1
   556	    p_value = (count_ge + 1) / (PERMUTATION_B + 1)
   557	
   558	    pre_mean = float(np.concatenate(groups[:k_obs+1]).mean())
   559	    post_mean = float(np.concatenate(groups[k_obs+1:]).mean())
   560	    degradation = post_mean < pre_mean
   561	
   562	    significant = p_value < DETECTOR_ALPHA
   563	    threshold = adoption_labels[k_obs + 1] if (significant and degradation) else None
   564	
   565	    # breakpoint bootstrap CI (path-level, fixed seed)
   566	    brng = np.random.RandomState(boot_seed % (2**31 - 1))
   567	    boot_thresholds = []
   568	    for _ in range(THRESHOLD_BOOT_B):
   569	        bg = [g[brng.randint(0, len(g), size=len(g))] for g in groups]
   570	        _, kb = _sup_wald(bg)
   571	        if kb is not None:
   572	            # direction check within replicate
   573	            pre_b = np.concatenate(bg[:kb+1]).mean()
   574	            post_b = np.concatenate(bg[kb+1:]).mean()
   575	            boot_thresholds.append(
   576	                adoption_labels[kb + 1] if post_b < pre_b else 'no_degradation')
   577	        else:
   578	            boot_thresholds.append('degenerate')
   579	    freq = {}
   580	    for tlbl in boot_thresholds:
   581	        freq[tlbl] = freq.get(tlbl, 0) + 1
   582	    freq = {k2: v / len(boot_thresholds) for k2, v in
   583	            sorted(freq.items(), key=lambda x: -x[1])}
   584	    # 80% interval over numeric breakpoint labels
   585	    numeric = sorted(
   586	        [float(t.rstrip('%')) for t in boot_thresholds
   587	         if isinstance(t, str) and t.endswith('%')])
   588	    if len(numeric) >= 10:
   589	        lo = numeric[int(0.10 * len(numeric))]
   590	        hi = numeric[min(int(0.90 * len(numeric)), len(numeric) - 1)]

 succeeded in 0ms:
60:We address this gap with an agent-based simulation in which the same Kyle-microstructure (constant $\lambda$, endogenous VIX) is exposed to a panel of strategy treatments (VT, TF, MR), three turnover-matched random-direction controls (RR\_VT, RR\_TF, RR\_MR) that replicate each treatment's per-step trading footprint while randomizing its direction, and a NoiseControl anchor verifying detector specificity. Detector inference is exogenous: a sup-Wald test with a flatness-calibrated permutation null over the adoption axis, plus path-bootstrap confidence intervals at each adoption level (M = 500 simulations per cell; \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}). Our contribution is threefold. First, we establish \emph{monotone strategy-specific erosion} as the empirical signature of VT crowding rather than a discrete tipping point: in the canonical cell ($\lambda = 0.005$, $\gamma = 200$), VT Sharpe declines from $0.510$ at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\%, the sup-Wald test rejects flatness in all five microstructure cells ($p = 0.001$ in every cell), adjacent path-bootstrap 95\% CIs separate from 40\% onward, and the maximum marginal degradation localizes to $(70\%, 100\%]$; the canonical ``70\% threshold'' survives only as a descriptive relative-drop level-crossing in 3 of 5 cells. Second, we identify the erosion mechanism to the \emph{systematic direction} of vol-feedback trading at VT's footprint scale: the turnover-matched random-direction control RR\_VT, which inherits VT's footprint without its directional rule, exhibits zero degradation in any cell (Sharpe in fact improves by $+0.06$ to $+0.09$ with adoption), isolating the externality to vol-feedback signal direction rather than to crowded flow at that scale. This control delivers a cleaner mechanism counterfactual than the no-strategy NoiseControl baseline of prior work; the scale qualifier matters, because at trend-following footprint scales the direction-randomized controls themselves erode significantly (\S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}). Third, we report \emph{joint robustness} of the qualitative monotone pattern across the five microstructure cells, which span $\pm$50\% perturbations of Kyle-$\lambda$ and VIX-feedback $\gamma$ around the canonical baseline. Matched-control evidence on TF and MR is constrained by extensive matched-input gate failures---the TF treatment is structurally loss-making in four of five cells and MR in all five at $s=10$---so family-level ordering claims are withdrawn and interpreted accordingly in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}. The fixed-vs-scaled-liquidity validation that featured in earlier work is retained as a methodological check (\S\ref{sec:validation}) and confirms that approximately half of the originally observed degradation is attributable to liquidity evaporation rather than to crowding per se. These results sharpen the policy-relevant question from ``at what adoption is the tipping point'' to ``how steep is the monotone erosion, and what identifies its mechanism.''
126:We organize the simulation evidence base into three layered phases. \textbf{Phase~1} (cross-treatment baseline at cell1 microstructure $\lambda = 0.005$, $\gamma = 200$) runs $M = 500$ independent Monte Carlo simulations across all four treatments (VT/TF/MR/NoiseControl) at the 7 adoption levels enumerated above---14{,}000 simulations in total, of which the 3{,}500-simulation VT slice reuses the earlier VT baseline run under identical settings (the other 10{,}500 are new) and supplies the standalone VT analysis (Tables~\ref{tab:main}--\ref{tab:market}). Bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals (2{,}000 replications) are reported for all key VT metrics. \textbf{Phase~2} (strategy-spec robustness) extends the comparison to TF and MR under a 12-cell scaling-by-window grid ($s \in \{1, 3, 5, 10\}$, $W \in \{10, 22, 60\}$) at 7 adoption levels with $M = 100$ MC per cell, totaling 16{,}800 simulations (Table~\ref{tab:scaling_window_matrix}). \textbf{Phase~2b} (microstructure OAT) crosses the four treatments under five $(\lambda, \gamma)$ cells (cell1 baseline plus $\lambda$/$\gamma$ each at $\pm 50\%$ of baseline; $\kappa$ is held fixed because this run, designed after Phase~1's then-current cross-treatment comparison, prioritizes the impact-and-feedback parameters most relevant to the Kyle mechanism) at four adoption levels ($\phi \in \{10\%, 30\%, 70\%, 100\%\}$) with $M = 200$ MC per (treatment, $\phi$) combination, totaling 16{,}000 simulations distributed across the 5 OAT cells (Table~\ref{tab:oat_robustness}). The three phases combine to 46{,}800 simulations spanning approximately 118 million agent-day observations. Finally, the \textbf{exogenous-detector redesign layer}---the evidence layer behind every headline claim in Section~3---resimulates all seven treatments and controls (VT, TF, MR, RR\_VT, RR\_TF, RR\_MR, NoiseControl) at $M = 500$ MC per (cell, adoption) point on a denser adoption grid: $\phi \in \{10, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 100\}\%$ in the canonical cell and $\phi \in \{10, 30, 50, 70, 100\}\%$ in each of the four perturbed cells, i.e.\ 27 cell--adoption combinations and $27 \times 7 \times 500 = 94{,}500$ simulations. The random-direction controls are \emph{coherent-block}: on each day a single random draw applies one common sign to the block's entire matched $|\Delta w|$ weight vector, so the control reproduces the treatment's aggregate, coordinated footprint---per-agent independent randomization would diversify away in aggregation and understate the footprint being matched. All simulation seeds are deterministic (a fixed base formula plus treatment-specific offsets that keep treatment and control seed streams disjoint), so every run is exactly reproducible. For each simulation we compute: annualized market volatility, strategy-treatment Sharpe ratio (annualized mean excess return over risk-free rate divided by annualized standard deviation of daily returns), maximum drawdown, excess kurtosis, skewness, flash-crash frequency (daily returns exceeding $3\sigma$), and VIX dynamics.
247:Matched-control evidence for TF and MR is reported in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy} subject to the matched-input applicability gate (\S\ref{sec:agents}); the gate outcome is extensive rather than marginal---the TF \emph{treatment} fails the baseline-Sharpe floor in four of five cells and the MR treatment in all five (Table~\ref{tab:tfmr_gate}), so the informative TF/MR matched-control comparison the design intended is largely unavailable at $s = 10$. The VT--RR\_VT comparison passes the gate in all five cells, providing the strongest single-strategy mechanism evidence in the paper.
300:The redesign layer's exogenous-detector evidence covers TF and MR alongside VT, and the honest summary is asymmetric: at the baseline parameterization ($s=10$, $W=22$), \emph{the model cannot deliver an informative matched-control comparison for TF or MR in most microstructure cells}, because the treatments themselves fail the matched-input applicability gate of \S\ref{sec:agents}. Table~\ref{tab:tfmr_gate} reports the complete gate outcomes together with the random-direction controls; this accounting supersedes the family-level ordering claims of earlier drafts, and we accordingly confine the paper's mechanism claims to VT.
330:Two findings follow. \emph{First, the gate excludes TF in four of five cells and MR in all five}: at $s = 10$ these treatments are structurally loss-making inside the model (baseline Sharpe $-0.69$ to $-5.49$), so any threshold estimated on them describes the behavior of a non-viable strategy rather than crowding of a viable one. The family-level ordering (``TF/MR thresholds at or below VT'') reported by earlier drafts and retained in the legacy tables below therefore rests on regimes the redesign layer itself rules inadmissible, and we withdraw it as evidence of class-level crowding. \emph{Second---and cutting against a fully strategy-specific reading---the random-direction controls at TF/MR footprint scale degrade significantly in every cell}: RR\_TF's sup-Wald test rejects flatness at $p = 0.001$ in 5 of 5 cells (descriptive level-crossings at 40--70\% adoption), and RR\_MR likewise. Because these controls trade coin-flip directions, their erosion cannot come from systematic signal direction; it comes from the sheer scale of the coordinated footprint ($|\Delta w|$ on the order of $1.5$ at $s=10$, versus VT's $0.004$--$0.008$). The correct scope statement for the paper's mechanism result is therefore: \emph{at VT's footprint scale}, erosion is identified to the systematic direction of vol-feedback trading (RR\_VT shows none); at footprint scales two orders of magnitude larger, direction-randomized coordinated flow erodes performance through price impact alone. The identification is footprint-scale-dependent, not an unconditional statement about crowded flow.
336:\caption{Critical adoption thresholds across treatments under three detector specifications. Source: Phase~1 cross-treatment run (10{,}500 new TF/MR/NoiseControl simulations plus the 3{,}500-simulation reused VT baseline slice; 14{,}000 in total); cross-detector recompute in the replication package.}
377:Within the legacy specification the TF threshold ranges from 20\% (aggressive scaling) to 70\% (mild scaling) and MR between 50\% and 70\%; we report these ranges as descriptive history only. Given the gate outcomes in Table~\ref{tab:tfmr_gate}, we no longer advance a family-level ordering claim from these sweeps. Section~\ref{sec:oat} extends the robustness analysis of the \emph{VT} result to the underlying microstructure parameters $\lambda$ and $\gamma$.
391:The VT erosion documented in \S\ref{sec:degradation_monotone} and the cross-strategy ordering TF/MR $\le$ VT documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy} could in principle be artifacts of a specific Kyle market-impact ($\lambda$) and VIX-feedback intensity ($\gamma$) choice. The threshold magnitudes reported in this subsection are outputs of the superseded Sharpe-only detector and are retained only as descriptive level-crossings for continuity with the earlier specification (\S\ref{sec:degradation_monotone}, footnote); the object whose robustness matters is the monotone shape and the cross-strategy ordering, not the crossing point. To rule this out, we run a one-at-a-time (OAT) sensitivity sweep over the two key microstructure parameters: $\lambda \in \{0.0025, 0.005, 0.0075\}$ and $\gamma \in \{100, 200, 300\}$, holding all other parameters at baseline. Each cell crosses the four treatments (VT, TF, MR, NoiseControl) at four adoption levels ($\phi \in \{10\%, 30\%, 70\%, 100\%\}$) with 200 Monte-Carlo simulations per (treatment, $\phi$) combination, yielding 16{,}000 simulations distributed across five OAT cells.
413:\item[b] Cell1-baseline TF/MR magnitudes (TF=30\%, MR=70\%) reported here use the Phase~2b OAT MC count $M = 200$. The same cell1 microstructure under the Phase~1 cross-treatment baseline ($M = 500$) yields TF=20\%, MR=20\% (Table~\ref{tab:cross_strategy_threshold}). The directional ordering TF/MR $\le$ VT is preserved under both MC settings; the magnitude gap reflects sampling variation at the (10\%, 20\%, 30\%) adoption-grid boundary, where the Sharpe-only detector's 70\% drop trigger is sensitive to MC sample size in the TF/MR baseline Sharpe. The 4 non-baseline OAT cells (cells 2--5) report internally consistent thresholds because they probe the qualitative direction of $\lambda$/$\gamma$ shifts rather than the precise grid-boundary magnitude.
418:Three findings emerge. First, \textbf{the descriptive level-crossing is internally consistent across our own runs}: the cell1 baseline VT crossing is 70\% under the Sharpe-only detector, the same value the standalone VT analysis (Table~\ref{tab:main}) and the cross-strategy reference in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy} produce under that detector. This is a within-detector consistency check, not external validation---the detector is calibrated on the same Sharpe path it then scores, and the exogenous sup-Wald redesign supersedes it. Second, the descriptive TF/MR rows behave consistently across the 5 OAT cells, but---per the gate accounting of \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}---these rows are computed in structurally loss-making treatment regimes, so we report them as continuity description only and draw no family-level ordering conclusion from them. Third, the \textbf{VT magnitude shifts directionally, not on a knife-edge}: only $\lambda$\,low (cell2) extends the VT threshold to 100\%, consistent with the Kyle-impact mechanism encoded in Eq.~(\ref{eq:price})---lower price impact means correlated VT selling moves prices less, so the feedback loop saturates at a higher adoption level. The $\gamma$ perturbations ($\pm 50\%$) produce \emph{no} shift in the VT threshold, indicating that VIX-feedback intensity is a non-binding parameter for the threshold magnitude. Both findings reinforce that the $\lambda$ dominance documented in earlier specifications was accurate for the VT result.
420:Combined with the strategy-spec robustness in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy} (12/12 scaling $\times$ window cells preserve the ordering), the joint robustness surface comprises 17 distinct parameter-perturbation checks (12 strategy-spec + 5 microstructure), all preserving TF/MR $\le$ VT. The critique that a single $(\lambda, \gamma)$ choice manufactures the 70\% threshold is therefore not supported by the data.
458:Seventh, the TF and MR scaling parameter $s = 10$ used in Phase~1 and the Phase~2b OAT cells is an aggressive choice, and it is the proximate cause of the extensive matched-input gate failures documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}: at $s = 10$ the TF and MR treatments are structurally loss-making in most cells, which is why we withdraw family-level ordering claims rather than presenting the legacy sweep (Table~\ref{tab:scaling_window_matrix}) as robustness. Real-world TF managers' effective scaling is heterogeneous and depends on volatility-of-volatility forecasts and capital-allocation rules \citep{moskowitz2012}; whether an informative TF/MR crowding comparison exists at realistic (lower) scaling intensities inside this model is an open question for future work, requiring a re-run in which the treatments themselves clear the applicability gate.
462:These limitations suggest that our quantitative ranges (roughly 20--100\% across the tested family and cells) should be interpreted as order-of-magnitude \emph{erosion profiles} rather than precise critical values. The design validation (Section~\ref{sec:validation}) demonstrates that even earlier threshold language was sensitive to modeling choices, while the exogenous-detector redesign shows the more robust object is the monotone shape itself and the strategy-class ordering within it. The emerging empirical literature on positive-feedback strategy crowding, including ongoing work on realized VT/TF/MR fund flows, will be essential for calibrating these erosion curves to real markets. The qualitative finding---that positive-feedback strategies exhibit strategy-specific monotone degradation whose severity depends on market impact and whose ordering (TF/MR $\le$ VT) is robust to the tested specification choices---is more robust than any single numerical threshold.
469:First, the \emph{VT} monotone-erosion pattern is robust to microstructure perturbation across 5 ($\lambda$, $\gamma$) cells (Table~\ref{tab:oat_robustness}). The legacy TF/MR ordering rows in that table are retained for continuity but carry the same caveat as \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}: they are computed in regimes that fail the matched-input gate (structurally loss-making treatments), so we do not count them as robustness evidence for a family-level claim. The $\pm 50\%$ perturbation range corresponds to the calibration uncertainty around our baseline cell rather than the full empirical span of $\lambda$ and $\gamma$ in the literature, which can vary by an order of magnitude across asset classes and time periods (Kyle-style $\lambda$ measures and inter-decadal VIX-realized-vol regression slopes both exhibit such variation in the empirical microstructure literature); a wider sweep is left to future work. The VT \emph{magnitude} does shift---from 70\% in 4/5 OAT cells to 100\% in cell2 ($\lambda$\,low)---but the shift is monotonic in $\lambda$ and consistent with the Kyle-impact mechanism encoded directly in Eq.~(\ref{eq:price}): lower price impact means correlated VT selling has a smaller per-trade price effect, and the feedback loop therefore saturates at a higher adoption level. The shift is a \emph{directional} magnitude response to a parameter that the model treats as exogenous, not a knife-edge.
471:Second, the NoiseControl falsifiability anchor never produces a degradation threshold (5/5 OAT cells, 12/12 strategy-spec cells, all 7 adoption levels). If our detector were mechanically picking up ``any sufficiently large coordinated agent block produces instability,'' NoiseControl would cross. It does not. The VT erosion structure is therefore tied to the positive-feedback property that VT encodes---and that NoiseControl lacks. We conclude that the VT erosion finding is mechanism-driven, not parameter-tuned: the canonical magnitude is itself the cell1-baseline output of a 5-cell OAT robustness suite, and the falsifiability anchor passes in 100\% of tested configurations. The legacy 12-cell TF/MR-vs-VT ordering matrix (Table~\ref{tab:scaling_window_matrix}) is retained for continuity only: computed under the superseded Sharpe-only detector in regimes that fail the matched-input gate, it is not counted as evidence for any family-level claim (\S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}).
478:This paper presents agent-based evidence that volatility targeting (VT) exhibits \emph{monotone erosion} under crowding, identified to the systematic direction of vol-feedback trading at VT's footprint scale. Using a Kyle (1985) market microstructure with 1{,}000 agents of heterogeneous types, 200 fixed noise traders, and 46{,}800 Monte Carlo simulations across the three paper phases (plus the 94{,}500-simulation exogenous-detector redesign layer that rewrites the detector narrative), we establish three claims. First, the robust object is not a discrete tipping point but a monotone decline in VT performance with adoption: in the canonical redesign cell, Sharpe falls from 0.510 at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\%, adjacent path-bootstrap confidence intervals separate from 40\% onward, and the exogenous sup-Wald detector rejects flatness without locating an internal break. Second, the matched-control evidence shows that VT's erosion is tied to the systematic direction of vol-feedback trading rather than to crowded turnover alone at VT's footprint scale; the cross-strategy extension (\S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}) instead yields an applicability finding---the TF and MR treatments are structurally loss-making at $s=10$ in four and five of five cells respectively---so family-level ordering claims are withdrawn rather than supported. Third, a design validation (\S\ref{sec:validation}) confirms that a substantial share of the na\"ive earlier degradation estimate came from liquidity evaporation rather than crowding per se, reinforcing the value of the more conservative fixed-liquidity design.

36:Volatility targeting (VT), trend-following (TF), and short-horizon mean-reversion (MR) share a procyclical structure: each strategy's position update is a non-trivial function of recent prices or volatility, so widespread adoption can in principle generate self-reinforcing trading flows. Whether the resulting strategy-performance degradation reflects a discrete crowding tipping point, and whether it is VT-specific or attributable to crowded flow in general, remains unsettled. We build an agent-based model with 1{,}000 agents of heterogeneous types, a Kyle (1985) market maker, endogenous VIX dynamics, and 200 fixed noise traders, and re-examine the threshold structure under matched microstructure with an exogenous sup-Wald detector and turnover-matched random-direction controls. Across 94{,}500 Monte Carlo simulations in the exogenous-detector redesign layer (27 cell--adoption combinations---seven adoption levels in the baseline cell and five in each of four perturbed cells---$\times$ 7 treatments/controls $\times$ 500 MC), three findings emerge. First, VT Sharpe declines \emph{monotonically} with adoption rather than crossing a discrete threshold: in the canonical cell ($\lambda = 0.005$, $\gamma = 200$), Sharpe falls from $0.510$ at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\%, with adjacent path-bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals separating from 40\% onward; the sup-Wald test rejects flatness in all five cells ($p = 0.001$ in every cell) but identifies no internal break, and the maximum marginal degradation concentrates in $(70\%, 100\%]$. Second, a turnover-matched random-direction control (RR\_VT) replicating VT's trading footprint without its vol-feedback direction exhibits no degradation in any cell; at VT's footprint scale, the erosion is therefore identified to the \emph{systematic direction} of vol-feedback trading rather than to crowded flow, providing a cleaner mechanism counterfactual than a no-strategy baseline (at trend-following footprint scales two orders of magnitude larger, direction-randomized controls do erode, so the identification is footprint-scale-dependent). Third, the canonical ``70\% threshold'' reported under a Sharpe-only detector survives only as a descriptive relative-drop level-crossing (drop $>70\%$) in 3 of 5 cells; under the exogenous, treatment-aware detector the interpretation is monotone erosion without a tipping point. The applicability gate excludes the TF treatment in four of five cells and MR in all five (structurally loss-making regimes), so cross-strategy family claims are withdrawn and the paper's mechanism evidence is confined to VT; our results sharpen the policy-relevant question from ``where is the tipping point'' to ``how steep is the monotone erosion and what identifies its mechanism.''
58:A critical gap remains: \emph{does correlated positive-feedback trading produce a discrete crowding tipping point, and---if degradation is observed---is it attributable to the systematic trading direction of the strategy or to crowded flow in general?} Existing analyses are either qualitative \citep{ecb2020}, focused on a single strategy without a counterfactual separating trading footprint from trading direction \citep{baltas2019}, or lack a quantitative threshold altogether. The empirical VT-alpha literature itself is contested---\citet{cederburg2020} and \citet{liu2019} question whether VT's Sharpe improvement survives realistic implementation costs, while \citet{barroso2021} find that volatility-managed market portfolios survive transaction costs in directions opposite to those questioned by \citet{cederburg2020}---but this debate is orthogonal to our question: we study the crowding externality \emph{conditional} on a strategy class being used, not whether any individual strategy delivers alpha. The mechanism-identification question we pose---separating crowded flow from systematic direction---requires a control that matches the offending strategy's trading footprint without inheriting its directional rule, a comparator absent from the prior literature.
60:We address this gap with an agent-based simulation in which the same Kyle-microstructure (constant $\lambda$, endogenous VIX) is exposed to a panel of strategy treatments (VT, TF, MR), three turnover-matched random-direction controls (RR\_VT, RR\_TF, RR\_MR) that replicate each treatment's per-step trading footprint while randomizing its direction, and a NoiseControl anchor verifying detector specificity. Detector inference is exogenous: a sup-Wald test with a flatness-calibrated permutation null over the adoption axis, plus path-bootstrap confidence intervals at each adoption level (M = 500 simulations per cell; \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}). Our contribution is threefold. First, we establish \emph{monotone strategy-specific erosion} as the empirical signature of VT crowding rather than a discrete tipping point: in the canonical cell ($\lambda = 0.005$, $\gamma = 200$), VT Sharpe declines from $0.510$ at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\%, the sup-Wald test rejects flatness in all five microstructure cells ($p = 0.001$ in every cell), adjacent path-bootstrap 95\% CIs separate from 40\% onward, and the maximum marginal degradation localizes to $(70\%, 100\%]$; the canonical ``70\% threshold'' survives only as a descriptive relative-drop level-crossing in 3 of 5 cells. Second, we identify the erosion mechanism to the \emph{systematic direction} of vol-feedback trading at VT's footprint scale: the turnover-matched random-direction control RR\_VT, which inherits VT's footprint without its directional rule, exhibits zero degradation in any cell (Sharpe in fact improves by $+0.06$ to $+0.09$ with adoption), isolating the externality to vol-feedback signal direction rather than to crowded flow at that scale. This control delivers a cleaner mechanism counterfactual than the no-strategy NoiseControl baseline of prior work; the scale qualifier matters, because at trend-following footprint scales the direction-randomized controls themselves erode significantly (\S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}). Third, we report \emph{joint robustness} of the qualitative monotone pattern across the five microstructure cells, which span $\pm$50\% perturbations of Kyle-$\lambda$ and VIX-feedback $\gamma$ around the canonical baseline. Matched-control evidence on TF and MR is constrained by extensive matched-input gate failures---the TF treatment is structurally loss-making in four of five cells and MR in all five at $s=10$---so family-level ordering claims are withdrawn and interpreted accordingly in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}. The fixed-vs-scaled-liquidity validation that featured in earlier work is retained as a methodological check (\S\ref{sec:validation}) and confirms that approximately half of the originally observed degradation is attributable to liquidity evaporation rather than to crowding per se. These results sharpen the policy-relevant question from ``at what adoption is the tipping point'' to ``how steep is the monotone erosion, and what identifies its mechanism.''
122:The model is designed to embed a positive feedback loop: when VT agents sell (because VIX rises), the negative order flow depresses prices via Eq.~\eqref{eq:price}, which increases realized volatility, which raises VIX via Eq.~\eqref{eq:vix}, which triggers further VT selling. This circular structure is a deliberate implementation of the \citet{brunnermeier2009} loss spiral applied to the VT context; it is a modeling assumption, not a simulation output. The strength of this feedback loop is proportional to $\phi$, and the simulation's purpose is to quantify how steeply the loop's performance consequences accumulate as adoption rises---not to locate an adoption level at which it switches on.
136:Table~\ref{tab:main} presents the core VT degradation results under fixed background liquidity ($N_{\text{noise}} = 200$). The pattern is best characterized as \emph{monotone, strategy-specific erosion}---a continuous decline in Sharpe with adoption rather than a discrete threshold crossing. Adjacent path-bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals separate from $\phi = 40\%$ onward (Table~\ref{tab:vt_monotone_curve}), the exogenous sup-Wald flatness test rejects $\phi$-invariant Sharpe in all five microstructure cells ($p = 0.001$ in every cell; full per-cell statistics in the replication package), and the maximum marginal degradation localizes to the $(70\%, 100\%]$ interval. The cell-level rejections survive multiple-testing discipline: across the redesign layer's 35 (cell, treatment) detector runs, a Bonferroni correction leaves $p = 0.001$ significant ($35 \times 0.001 = 0.035 < 0.05$), while marginal readings at $p = 0.003$ would not survive and are not relied upon.\footnote{An earlier version of this paper, calibrated against a Sharpe-only detector that itself used adoption-level performance to define both the threshold and the falsification criterion, reported a discrete ``70\% tipping point.'' The exogenous-detector resimulation ($M = 500$, documented in the replication package) establishes that the sup-Wald rejection of flatness is consistent with smooth monotone decline and does not localize an internal break: the permutation null tests $\phi$-invariance, not the absence of a break, so the resulting $p = 0.001$ in every cell identifies non-flatness but not a discrete threshold. The headline ``70\%'' figure survives only as a descriptive level-crossing (relative Sharpe drop $>70\%$) in 3 of 5 microstructure cells and is not the output of a structural break detector. The exogenous-detector narrative supersedes the earlier tipping-point framing throughout this paper.}
138:In the canonical cell ($\lambda = 0.005$, $\gamma = 200$, $M = 500$ Monte Carlo paths), VT Sharpe falls from $0.510$ at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\% (a $-0.78$ absolute change spanning the adoption axis), passing through $0.481$ at 30\%, $0.405$ at 40\%, $0.338$ at 50\%, $0.203$ at 60\%, and $0.091$ at 70\%. The 30--50\% decline that the earlier Sharpe-only narrative attributed to a ``safe-zone-to-tipping'' transition is, under the exogenous detector with denser adoption grid, the middle portion of an already-significant monotone slope; the 70--100\% interval contains the steepest single-step drop ($0.091 \to -0.271$) but the path-bootstrap CIs of all adjacent levels from 40\% onward are non-overlapping, so the steepness pattern is one of accelerating monotone erosion rather than knife-edge transition. Table~\ref{tab:vt_monotone_curve} reports the full canonical-cell curve.
162:\item Path-bootstrap CIs use 2{,}000 resamples within each adoption cell drawn over MC simulation paths (not within-path days), so the reported widths reflect between-simulation Sharpe dispersion. The 10\%--30\% step is the only adjacent pair whose CIs overlap; from 40\% onward, no adjacent CI pair overlaps, providing statistical evidence of continuous monotone decline. The grid spacing 70\%--100\% is the widest in the adoption axis (30 pp versus 10 pp elsewhere); the $-0.362$ step there should be read as the maximum marginal degradation \emph{rate per unit adoption} only after dividing by the spacing, which yields a per-10pp rate of $-0.121$ comparable to the 60\%--70\% step ($-0.112$ per 10pp) rather than a discrete jump.
167:Table~\ref{tab:main} provides the legacy results table summarising VT strategy and market outcomes on the original adoption grid (0, 10, 20, 30, 50, 70, 100\%); the qualitative pattern there---essentially flat below 30\%, sharp drop to negative Sharpe by 100\%---remains correct under the new framing, but should be read as a sparse-grid view of the monotone erosion documented in Table~\ref{tab:vt_monotone_curve} rather than as evidence of a discrete tipping point. The narrow Sharpe interval at $\phi = 10$--$30\%$ in Table~\ref{tab:main} corresponds to the leftmost segment of the monotone curve where decline rate per unit adoption is still small; the sharp drop from 50\% to 70\% in Table~\ref{tab:main} reflects the same monotone slope steepening through the dense-grid 40\%, 50\%, 60\% intermediate levels shown in Table~\ref{tab:vt_monotone_curve}.
194:\item[a] At 100\% adoption, the moderate flash crash frequency relative to 70\% reflects a measurement artifact: the standard deviation used to define the $3\sigma$ threshold is itself severely inflated (annualized vol of 35\%), masking extreme events. We flag this in §\ref{sec:results} ¶2 to forestall mis-reading the row.
295:The 10\% versus 30\% comparison yields $t = 0.05$ ($p = 0.96$): the two levels are statistically indistinguishable, consistent with the shallow slope at the left end of the curve (Table~\ref{tab:vt_monotone_curve}). The 50\% versus 10\% comparison yields $t = 7.12$ ($p < 0.001$), far exceeding the Harvey threshold, so the accumulated decline by mid-adoption is not simulation noise. The 70\% versus 50\% comparison yields $t = 16.94$ ($p < 0.001$, with $\bar{S}_{50\%} = 0.336$ vs.\ $\bar{S}_{70\%} = 0.084$), the largest of the three: the slope is steeper in the upper half of the adoption axis than in the lower half. That ordering of $t$-statistics is what an accelerating monotone decline predicts; it is equally consistent with, and therefore cannot discriminate in favor of, a discrete break.

Section~\ref{sec:oat} extends the robustness analysis of the \emph{VT} result to the underlying microstructure parameters $\lambda$ and $\gamma$.
381:We emphasize that the positive feedback mechanism is \emph{encoded in the model}, not discovered from the simulation output. The VIX responds to realized volatility (Eq.~\ref{eq:vix}), VT agents respond to VIX, and their trading affects prices (Eq.~\ref{eq:price}), which in turn affects realized volatility. This circular structure is a deliberate implementation of the \citet{brunnermeier2009} liquidity spiral framework. What the simulation \emph{does} reveal is the \emph{quantitative erosion profile}: the feedback loop's performance consequences are small below 30\% adoption, are already unambiguous by 50\% ($-0.17$ Sharpe relative to the 10\% level), and continue to steepen through 100\% (Table~\ref{tab:vt_monotone_curve}), with no adoption level at which the loop switches on. Making the feedback explicit, the aggregate VT order flow on day $t$ is $\phi N \Delta w_t^{\text{VT}}$, where the VT weight change uses the capped rule from Eq.~(\ref{eq:vt_rule}),
391:The VT erosion documented in \S\ref{sec:degradation_monotone} and the cross-strategy ordering TF/MR $\le$ VT documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy} could in principle be artifacts of a specific Kyle market-impact ($\lambda$) and VIX-feedback intensity ($\gamma$) choice. The threshold magnitudes reported in this subsection are outputs of the superseded Sharpe-only detector and are retained only as descriptive level-crossings for continuity with the earlier specification (\S\ref{sec:degradation_monotone}, footnote); the object whose robustness matters is the monotone shape and the cross-strategy ordering, not the crossing point. To rule this out, we run a one-at-a-time (OAT) sensitivity sweep over the two key microstructure parameters: $\lambda \in \{0.0025, 0.005, 0.0075\}$ and $\gamma \in \{100, 200, 300\}$, holding all other parameters at baseline. Each cell crosses the four treatments (VT, TF, MR, NoiseControl) at four adoption levels ($\phi \in \{10\%, 30\%, 70\%, 100\%\}$) with 200 Monte-Carlo simulations per (treatment, $\phi$) combination, yielding 16{,}000 simulations distributed across five OAT cells.
412:\item[a] MR null in cell3 reflects saturation in a structurally loss-making regime: at $\lambda = 0.0075$, the MR 10\%-baseline Sharpe is already $-5.56$, and no further deterioration crosses the Sharpe-only detector's drop $> 70\%$ threshold (which would require Sharpe $< -9.45$); no sign-flip occurs because Sharpe stays negative across all tested adoption levels. Under the rank encoding used in our verdict-classification logic, the null is treated as MR threshold $\ge$ VT threshold (saturation is strictly stronger than ``crosses just above'' in the H1+ ordering).
413:\item[b] Cell1-baseline TF/MR magnitudes (TF=30\%, MR=70\%) reported here use the Phase~2b OAT MC count $M = 200$. The same cell1 microstructure under the Phase~1 cross-treatment baseline ($M = 500$) yields TF=20\%, MR=20\% (Table~\ref{tab:cross_strategy_threshold}). The directional ordering TF/MR $\le$ VT is preserved under both MC settings; the magnitude gap reflects sampling variation at the (10\%, 20\%, 30\%) adoption-grid boundary, where the Sharpe-only detector's 70\% drop trigger is sensitive to MC sample size in the TF/MR baseline Sharpe. The 4 non-baseline OAT cells (cells 2--5) report internally consistent thresholds because they probe the qualitative direction of $\lambda$/$\gamma$ shifts rather than the precise grid-boundary magnitude.
418:Three findings emerge. First, \textbf{the descriptive level-crossing is internally consistent across our own runs}: the cell1 baseline VT crossing is 70\% under the Sharpe-only detector, the same value the standalone VT analysis (Table~\ref{tab:main}) and the cross-strategy reference in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy} produce under that detector. This is a within-detector consistency check, not external validation---the detector is calibrated on the same Sharpe path it then scores, and the exogenous sup-Wald redesign supersedes it. Second, the descriptive TF/MR rows behave consistently across the 5 OAT cells, but---per the gate accounting of \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}---these rows are computed in structurally loss-making treatment regimes, so we report them as continuity description only and draw no family-level ordering conclusion from them. Third, the \textbf{VT magnitude shifts directionally, not on a knife-edge}: only $\lambda$\,low (cell2) extends the VT threshold to 100\%, consistent with the Kyle-impact mechanism encoded in Eq.~(\ref{eq:price})---lower price impact means correlated VT selling moves prices less, so the feedback loop saturates at a higher adoption level. The $\gamma$ perturbations ($\pm 50\%$) produce \emph{no} shift in the VT threshold, indicating that VIX-feedback intensity is a non-binding parameter for the threshold magnitude. Both findings reinforce that the $\lambda$ dominance documented in earlier specifications was accurate for the VT result.
420:Combined with the strategy-spec robustness in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy} (12/12 scaling $\times$ window cells preserve the ordering), the joint robustness surface comprises 17 distinct parameter-perturbation checks (12 strategy-spec + 5 microstructure), all preserving TF/MR $\le$ VT. The critique that a single $(\lambda, \gamma)$ choice manufactures the 70\% threshold is therefore not supported by the data.
436:The main policy implication is not that regulators should anchor surveillance to a single cliff-like adoption threshold, but that they should monitor the \emph{slope} of strategy-specific erosion as adoption broadens. Our exogenous-detector redesign shows that the dominant empirical pattern is monotone degradation with strategy-class heterogeneity: VT deteriorates steadily in all five microstructure cells, while TF/MR evidence is weaker and in part bounded by pathological-regime gates. This shifts the relevant macroprudential question from ``has the system crossed 70\%?'' to ``is adoption rising into the part of the curve where marginal degradation steepens materially?'' In practice, that points toward monitoring the combined footprint of positive-feedback strategies, but interpreting the evidence as a gradual erosion map rather than a bright-line trigger. The ESRB and FSOC could therefore treat adoption metrics as one input into macroprudential surveillance alongside broader crowding measures \citep{baltas2019}, with particular attention to whether the dominant deployed class is one whose matched-control evidence isolates degradation to systematic signal direction.
440:For practitioners, the practical lesson is similarly about \emph{erosion rates}, not discrete cliffs. The canonical VT cell still shows a relatively shallow decline from 10\% to 30\% adoption and a much steeper deterioration from 40\% onward, so implementation risk rises as crowding builds even before any stylized ``threshold'' label would be attached. This suggests that managers should monitor proxy indicators such as the correlation between VIX changes and equity-fund flows, volatility-linked AUM concentration, and anomalous intraday reversals during VIX spikes, but interpret these as markers of progressively worsening crowding conditions rather than as alarms for a single regime switch. The matched-control evidence also matters operationally: if degradation is tied to the \emph{direction} of vol-feedback trades rather than to turnover alone, then diversification across execution timing or footprint without altering the common directional rule may not be sufficient protection.
460:Eighth, we treat VT, TF, and MR as discrete strategy classes, but real positive-feedback strategies sit on a continuum: risk-parity overlays blend volatility-targeting with cross-asset rebalancing, target-volatility-overlay funds combine VT scaling with momentum filters, and trend-overlay leverage products embed both TF and VT signal structures. Future work should test whether arbitrary mixtures of feedback signals share the same threshold structure documented here, since real-world adoption of positive-feedback strategies likely takes the form of mixed-signal products rather than pure-class agents.
464:\subsection{Reviewer-Anticipated Objection: Knife-Edge Critique Addressed}
467:A reviewer might reasonably argue that the VT erosion is an artifact of specific parameter tuning---a ``knife-edge'' result that vanishes under perturbation. The concern has less purchase against the monotone framing than it had against the earlier tipping-point framing---a discrete threshold is a knife-edge object by construction, whereas a monotone slope is not---but the finding still has to survive perturbation. Two considerations rebut the concern, in order of binding strength.
469:First, the \emph{VT} monotone-erosion pattern is robust to microstructure perturbation across 5 ($\lambda$, $\gamma$) cells (Table~\ref{tab:oat_robustness}). The legacy TF/MR ordering rows in that table are retained for continuity but carry the same caveat as \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}: they are computed in regimes that fail the matched-input gate (structurally loss-making treatments), so we do not count them as robustness evidence for a family-level claim. The $\pm 50\%$ perturbation range corresponds to the calibration uncertainty around our baseline cell rather than the full empirical span of $\lambda$ and $\gamma$ in the literature, which can vary by an order of magnitude across asset classes and time periods (Kyle-style $\lambda$ measures and inter-decadal VIX-realized-vol regression slopes both exhibit such variation in the empirical microstructure literature); a wider sweep is left to future work. The VT \emph{magnitude} does shift---from 70\% in 4/5 OAT cells to 100\% in cell2 ($\lambda$\,low)---but the shift is monotonic in $\lambda$ and consistent with the Kyle-impact mechanism encoded directly in Eq.~(\ref{eq:price}): lower price impact means correlated VT selling has a smaller per-trade price effect, and the feedback loop therefore saturates at a higher adoption level. The shift is a \emph{directional} magnitude response to a parameter that the model treats as exogenous, not a knife-edge.
478:This paper presents agent-based evidence that volatility targeting (VT) exhibits \emph{monotone erosion} under crowding, identified to the systematic direction of vol-feedback trading at VT's footprint scale. Using a Kyle (1985) market microstructure with 1{,}000 agents of heterogeneous types, 200 fixed noise traders, and 46{,}800 Monte Carlo simulations across the three paper phases (plus the 94{,}500-simulation exogenous-detector redesign layer that rewrites the detector narrative), we establish three claims. First, the robust object is not a discrete tipping point but a monotone decline in VT performance with adoption: in the canonical redesign cell, Sharpe falls from 0.510 at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\%, adjacent path-bootstrap confidence intervals separate from 40\% onward, and the exogenous sup-Wald detector rejects flatness without locating an internal break. Second, the matched-control evidence shows that VT's erosion is tied to the systematic direction of vol-feedback trading rather than to crowded turnover alone at VT's footprint scale; the cross-strategy extension (\S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}) instead yields an applicability finding---the TF and MR treatments are structurally loss-making at $s=10$ in four and five of five cells respectively---so family-level ordering claims are withdrawn rather than supported. Third, a design validation (\S\ref{sec:validation}) confirms that a substantial share of the na\"ive earlier degradation estimate came from liquidity evaporation rather than crowding per se, reinforcing the value of the more conservative fixed-liquidity design.

36:Volatility targeting (VT), trend-following (TF), and short-horizon mean-reversion (MR) share a procyclical structure: each strategy's position update is a non-trivial function of recent prices or volatility, so widespread adoption can in principle generate self-reinforcing trading flows. Whether the resulting strategy-performance degradation reflects a discrete crowding tipping point, and whether it is VT-specific or attributable to crowded flow in general, remains unsettled. We build an agent-based model with 1{,}000 agents of heterogeneous types, a Kyle (1985) market maker, endogenous VIX dynamics, and 200 fixed noise traders, and re-examine the threshold structure under matched microstructure with an exogenous sup-Wald detector and turnover-matched random-direction controls. Across 94{,}500 Monte Carlo simulations in the exogenous-detector redesign layer (27 cell--adoption combinations---seven adoption levels in the baseline cell and five in each of four perturbed cells---$\times$ 7 treatments/controls $\times$ 500 MC), three findings emerge. First, VT Sharpe declines \emph{monotonically} with adoption rather than crossing a discrete threshold: in the canonical cell ($\lambda = 0.005$, $\gamma = 200$), Sharpe falls from $0.510$ at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\%, with adjacent path-bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals separating from 40\% onward; the sup-Wald test rejects flatness in all five cells ($p = 0.001$ in every cell) but identifies no internal break, and the maximum marginal degradation concentrates in $(70\%, 100\%]$. Second, a turnover-matched random-direction control (RR\_VT) replicating VT's trading footprint without its vol-feedback direction exhibits no degradation in any cell; at VT's footprint scale, the erosion is therefore identified to the \emph{systematic direction} of vol-feedback trading rather than to crowded flow, providing a cleaner mechanism counterfactual than a no-strategy baseline (at trend-following footprint scales two orders of magnitude larger, direction-randomized controls do erode, so the identification is footprint-scale-dependent). Third, the canonical ``70\% threshold'' reported under a Sharpe-only detector survives only as a descriptive relative-drop level-crossing (drop $>70\%$) in 3 of 5 cells; under the exogenous, treatment-aware detector the interpretation is monotone erosion without a tipping point. The applicability gate excludes the TF treatment in four of five cells and MR in all five (structurally loss-making regimes), so cross-strategy family claims are withdrawn and the paper's mechanism evidence is confined to VT; our results sharpen the policy-relevant question from ``where is the tipping point'' to ``how steep is the monotone erosion and what identifies its mechanism.''
56:Despite individual-level benefits, all three strategy classes embed a fundamentally procyclical mechanism: when volatility rises (or trends turn, or signals saturate), all adopters simultaneously rebalance in the same direction, creating correlated trading pressure. The crowding risk literature warns of such procyclical dynamics---\citet{baltas2019} documents crowding effects in alternative risk premia, the European Central Bank \citep[ECB,][]{ecb2020} explicitly flagged VT procyclicality as a source of market fragility, and \citet{cont2000} build an agent-based herd-behavior model in which correlated demand generates fat-tailed returns. The closely related fire-sale literature \citep{greenwood2011} shows that forced selling by one investor class concentrates price impact on overlapping holdings and can amplify subsequent declines, a mechanism we view as the continuous-time counterpart of the adoption-axis erosion we estimate. None of these studies, however, provides a \emph{strategy-class-by-strategy-class} quantitative threshold. Several commentators draw parallels to the portfolio insurance strategies implicated in the 1987 crash \citep{gennotte1990}. \citet{bookstaber2014} use agent-based modeling to study financial system fragility broadly, while \citet{lebaron2006} and \citet{danielsson2012} study feedback-driven market dynamics; applications that \emph{explicitly compare} VT crowding against TF or MR crowding under identical microstructure are absent.
60:We address this gap with an agent-based simulation in which the same Kyle-microstructure (constant $\lambda$, endogenous VIX) is exposed to a panel of strategy treatments (VT, TF, MR), three turnover-matched random-direction controls (RR\_VT, RR\_TF, RR\_MR) that replicate each treatment's per-step trading footprint while randomizing its direction, and a NoiseControl anchor verifying detector specificity. Detector inference is exogenous: a sup-Wald test with a flatness-calibrated permutation null over the adoption axis, plus path-bootstrap confidence intervals at each adoption level (M = 500 simulations per cell; \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}). Our contribution is threefold. First, we establish \emph{monotone strategy-specific erosion} as the empirical signature of VT crowding rather than a discrete tipping point: in the canonical cell ($\lambda = 0.005$, $\gamma = 200$), VT Sharpe declines from $0.510$ at 10\% adoption to $-0.271$ at 100\%, the sup-Wald test rejects flatness in all five microstructure cells ($p = 0.001$ in every cell), adjacent path-bootstrap 95\% CIs separate from 40\% onward, and the maximum marginal degradation localizes to $(70\%, 100\%]$; the canonical ``70\% threshold'' survives only as a descriptive relative-drop level-crossing in 3 of 5 cells. Second, we identify the erosion mechanism to the \emph{systematic direction} of vol-feedback trading at VT's footprint scale: the turnover-matched random-direction control RR\_VT, which inherits VT's footprint without its directional rule, exhibits zero degradation in any cell (Sharpe in fact improves by $+0.06$ to $+0.09$ with adoption), isolating the externality to vol-feedback signal direction rather than to crowded flow at that scale. This control delivers a cleaner mechanism counterfactual than the no-strategy NoiseControl baseline of prior work; the scale qualifier matters, because at trend-following footprint scales the direction-randomized controls themselves erode significantly (\S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}). Third, we report \emph{joint robustness} of the qualitative monotone pattern across the five microstructure cells, which span $\pm$50\% perturbations of Kyle-$\lambda$ and VIX-feedback $\gamma$ around the canonical baseline. Matched-control evidence on TF and MR is constrained by extensive matched-input gate failures---the TF treatment is structurally loss-making in four of five cells and MR in all five at $s=10$---so family-level ordering claims are withdrawn and interpreted accordingly in \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}. The fixed-vs-scaled-liquidity validation that featured in earlier work is retained as a methodological check (\S\ref{sec:validation}) and confirms that approximately half of the originally observed degradation is attributable to liquidity evaporation rather than to crowding per se. These results sharpen the policy-relevant question from ``at what adoption is the tipping point'' to ``how steep is the monotone erosion, and what identifies its mechanism.''
95:with the same baseline $s = 10$, $W = 22$. The negative coefficient captures the short-horizon reversal documented by \citet{lehmann1990} and \citet{lo1990}. In our agent-based environment, MR's positive-feedback channel arises asymmetrically: when prices fall, the strategy buys, but if a sufficiently large fraction of agents trade in the same direction, the buying pressure itself can push prices high enough to trigger a reverse-direction signal cascade. This produces episodic price-collapse-and-recovery behavior at intermediate adoption (e.g.\ final prices on the order of $10^{-23}$ at $\phi = 30\%$ in 500/500 simulations from the Phase~1 run --- a legitimate simulation finding rather than a numerical artifact, documented in the threshold caveats of \S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}).
97:\paragraph{NoiseControl (NC).} Falsifiability anchor with deterministic weight $w^{\text{NC}}_t = 0.5$. The 0.5 weight matches the noise-trader baseline (mean weight $0.5$ from $\Delta w \sim N(0, 0.02)$ random walk anchored at 0.5), ensuring NoiseControl's mean order-flow contribution matches that of the noise traders rather than that of BH agents (whose constant unit weight would inflate aggregate order flow under high $\phi$). This is the strictest falsifier choice: if even matching-noise behavior produced a threshold, our positive-feedback claim would be undermined. The NoiseControl treatment replaces VT/TF/MR agents with constant-weight agents whose order flow is statistically indistinguishable from the noise-trader background. Under the null hypothesis that crowding is a generic mechanical artifact of large coordinated trading---rather than a positive-feedback property---NoiseControl should still produce a critical adoption threshold; under our maintained hypothesis it should not. The Phase~1 run (\S\ref{sec:cross_strategy}) confirms NoiseControl produces no detectable threshold across $\phi \in \{0\%, \ldots, 100\%\}$, establishing that the cross-treatment threshold differences reflect the strategies' positive-feedback structure rather than their headcount.
99:\paragraph{Random-direction matched controls (RR\_VT, RR\_TF, RR\_MR).} The NoiseControl anchor establishes that headcount alone does not produce crowding, but it cannot identify whether the residual treatment-specific erosion is driven by (i) the systematic \emph{direction} of vol-feedback / momentum / mean-reversion signals, or (ii) the sheer correlated \emph{footprint} of high-turnover trading regardless of direction. To separate these two channels, we introduce a stronger counterfactual that holds the treatment's trading footprint fixed but randomizes its directional rule. For each treatment $X \in \{\text{VT, TF, MR}\}$, the matched-control RR\_$X$ at adoption $\phi$ inherits, at every step $t$, the per-step weight-change \emph{magnitude} $|\Delta w_t^X|$ and rebalancing frequency $\text{freq}^X$ realized by the corresponding $X$ treatment in a paired simulation, then applies the sign of $\Delta w_t$ from an independent Bernoulli$(0.5)$ draw, retaining the original clip rail $[0, 1.5]$. Under the maintained hypothesis that crowding is mechanism-driven (a function of systematic positive-feedback direction), RR\_$X$ should exhibit no degradation despite matching $X$'s footprint; under the null that any large coordinated turnover destabilizes the market, RR\_$X$ should reproduce $X$'s degradation. RR controls deliver a cleaner mechanism counterfactual than NoiseControl because the comparator is endogenous to the treatment rather than a fixed deterministic baseline.
